# From Numerical Simulators of PDEs to Neural Emulators and 

# Back 

Felix Matteo Köhler 

Vollständiger Abdruck der von der TUM School of Computation, Information and Technology der Technischen Universität München zur Erlangung eines Doktors der Naturwissenschaften (Dr. rer. nat.) genehmigten Dissertation. 

Vorsitz: Prof. Dr. Daniel Cremers Prüfende der Dissertation: 

1. Prof. Dr.-Ing. Nils Thürey 2. Assistant Prof. Dr. William de Almeida Gilpin (schriftliche Beurteilung) 2. Prof. Dr. Riccardo Marin (mündliche Prüfung) 

Die Dissertation wurde am 21.04.2026 bei der Technischen Universität München eingereicht und durch die TUM School of Computation, Information and Technology am 02.07.2026 angenommen. arXiv:2608.24547v1 [cs.LG] 25 Aug 2026 Abstract 

Simulation is central to modern engineering and science, but the cost of numerical solvers for par-tial differential equations (PDEs) remains a bottleneck whenever fast or many-query evaluations are required. Neural emulators trained on solver-generated data promise significant speedups, yet they are usually framed as opaque alternatives to the very methods that produce their training signal. This thesis argues the two paradigms are more alike than different: neural architectures mirror classical discretizations, their errors are amenable to the same spectral analysis, and insight flows profitably in both directions. We approach the relationship by disentangling the multiple roles a solver plays in the emulator learning pipeline. Mode-wise Fourier analysis then provides a common language in which solver errors, architectural inductive biases, and training objectives can all be read off simultaneously. Taken together, this allows synthesizing three contributions. (1) APEBench , a comprehensive benchmarking suite for autoregressive neural emulators of PDEs that uses fast differentiable pseudo-spectral solvers in JAX. (2) Progressively Refined Differentiable Physics , an investigation of the effect of unconverged solvers on surrogate training. (3) Neural Emulator Superiority , an analysis of the influence of numerical errors and architectural inductive biases. 

iii Zusammenfassung 

Simulationen sind ein zentrales Werkzeug in Ingenieurwesen und Wissenschaft. Dennoch bleiben die Kosten numerischer Löser für partielle Differentialgleichungen (PDGl) ein limitierender Faktor, insbesondere wenn schnelle oder vielfach wiederholte Auswertungen erforderlich sind. Neuronale Emulatoren, die auf den synthetischen Daten solcher Simulatoren trainiert werden, versprechen erhebliche Geschwindigkeitsgewinne. Sie werden jedoch häufig als undurchsichtige Alternative zu genau jenen Verfahren dargestellt, aus denen ihr Trainingssignal stammt. In dieser Arbeit argumentieren wir, dass beide Paradigmen einander ähnlicher sind, als es zunächst scheint: Neuronale Architekturen spiegeln klassische Diskretisierungsverfahren wider, ihre Fehler lassen sich mit derselben spektralen Analyse untersuchen, und Erkenntnisse fließen gewinnbringend in beide Richtungen. Wir nähern uns dieser Verbindung, indem wir die vielfältigen Rollen, die ein numerischer Löser im Trainingsprozess eines Emulators einnimmt, klar voneinander trennen. Eine modenweise Fourier-Analyse bietet dann eine gemeinsame Sprache, in der sich Löserfehler, architekturelle induktive Biases und Lernziele gleichzeitig ablesen lassen. Dieses Gerüst ermöglicht die Synthese dreier wissenschaftlicher Arbeiten. (1) APEBench , eine umfassende Benchmark-Suite zur Evaluierung autoregressiver neuronaler Emulatoren von PDGl, basierend auf schnellen, differenzierbaren pseudo-spektralen Lösern in JAX. (2) Progressively Refined Differentiable Physics , eine Untersuchung der Auswirkung nicht vollständig konvergierter Löser auf das Emulatortraining. (3) Neural Emulator Superiority , eine Analyse des Einflusses numerischer Fehler und architektureller induktiver Biases.

xv Chapter 1 

# Introduction 

# 1.1 The Importance and Computational Challenge of Simulating Partial Differential Equations 

Our understanding of nature and engineering increasingly depends on running large-scale sim-ulations of physical phenomena (Bauer et al., 2015). These approximations to fundamental descriptions of reality allow gaining deep insights into processes that would be hard to investigate with real-world experiments. For example, it allows understanding how materials behave at a subatomic level (Car and Parrinello, 1985), how objects deform under load (Zienkiewicz et al., 2013), how aerodynamic a car design is (Ferziger et al., 2020), how to animate special effects in video games or feature films realistically (Bridson, 2015), or how to forecast the weather (Bauer et al., 2015). Across all those scales, there is a fundamental language that describes these processes, the one of partial differential equations (PDEs). Those describe a constraint that a solution field ,e.g., a fluid velocity field, must fulfill to be in a valid state. Classically, simulation is the procedure of finding approximate solutions by symbolically manipulating those continuous descriptions, deriving efficient discretizations from first principles and implementing them as a computer program. While first approaches can be traced back more than 100 years ago (Courant et al., 1928; Richardson, 1922), modern simulation science grew alongside the growth of digital computers (Gustafsson, 2018) 1. Consistent with the increase of computational power (Strohmaier et al., 2015) and improvements in algorithms (Rüde et al., 2018), ever larger and more sophisticated simulations became feasible. Of particular interest is the simulation of problems in a three-dimensional space with an additional time axis, as those most closely resemble reality (Pope, 2001). While there is an ever-increasing set of problems we can simulate, the required time for doing so can be infeasibly high. Especially because the difficulty is not just simulating a problem once , but having the simulation be part of a larger experimental loop. This could include a human design engineer who iteratively tweaks parts of the geometry and requires feedback from the simulation. It could also be an automated optimization process, which might require on the order of thousands of simulations to converge (Forrester et al., 2008). 

# 1.2 Classical Numerical Methods and their Surrogation 

To reduce the computational cost of simulations, researchers devised surrogates to full-fledged simulations. Oftentimes, they purposefully leave out certain details that are deemed less important for usage in the larger design loop. There are typically three types of building surrogates: 1. One can use simpler models , e.g., finding a simplification in two or one dimension, or using a simpler constitutive model that is easier to integrate. 2. One can truncate numerics of the simulation, e.g., by prematurely terminating an iterative process or under-resolving a phenomenon.

> h

sampled from a distribution Ih are rolled out via the trajectory operator T to produce data trajectories Dh, which are used to train and test the neural emulator fθ .

3. One can devise data-driven surrogates that attempt to understand patterns from concrete examples of the simulation. While in approaches (1) and (2), we keep control over numerical guarantees and first principles, in (3) we often purposefully loosen them. Early approaches following (3) were called Reduced-Order Models, oftentimes using Proper Orthogonal Decomposition (POD 2) (Benner et al., 2015). In its core, this approach tried to compress a high-resolution state space into a more manageable representation in which one can efficiently integrate in time. It tried to approximate the physics’ time derivative but left the time integration to classical approaches. A recent trend is to replace both the physics and the numerics by a machine learning model (Guo et al., 2016; Li et al., 2021). As such, one finds surrogates that, in their most extreme forms, are fully data-driven, without any numerical principles. Since such approaches are mostly based on deep learning, we will refer to them as neural emulators . Oftentimes, they handle time sequentially, i.e., they predict next frames based on previous ones autoregressively (Brandstetter et al., 2022). Hence, a core theme of this thesis will be autoregressive neural emulators .

# 1.3 Thesis Statement and Research Questions 

The prevalent theme of training autoregressive neural emulators is to use synthetic data produced with conventional numerical methods; see Figure 1.1 for an overview. This is different from many other domains of machine learning that use large-scale empirically obtained datasets like annotated images or text corpora from the web (Brown et al., 2020; Deng et al., 2009). Since the ultimate goal of creating surrogates is finding a tool that is more advantageously positioned on the ever-present accuracy-speed trade-off in the approximate solutions of PDEs, this creates an interesting interplay between emulators and simulators. Moreover, the neural architectures being used as surrogates have structural and functional similarities with certain numerical algorithms (Alt et al., 2023; Long et al., 2018). These two aspects, the student-teacher relationship and the functional similarity, raise interesting fundamental questions that are central to this thesis, leading to the statement: Numerical simulators of PDEs and neural emulators share significant similarities and a deep understanding of the holistic surrogate-learning pipeline can inform good decisions in both directions. Concretely, we approach this from three angles. First, we use tools from numerical analysis, in particular Fourier-spectral error analysis, to understand why certain emulators succeed or fail, and why they can sometimes exceed the fidelity of the solver that generated their training data.

Figure 1.2.: Overview of the three publications comprising this thesis. APEBench provides the benchmarking framework that enables systematic measurement of neural PDE emulators. In Emulator Superiority, we study the effect of numerical errors on surrogate quality with the surprising finding that they can sometimes exceed their training data fidelity. With PRDP, we demonstrate how to train emulators efficiently using progressively refined differentiable solvers. The bidirectional arc captures the thesis title: insights flow from numerical solvers to neural emulators and back. 

Second, we show how the solver’s role in the training loop can be exploited: full convergence of iterative solvers is unnecessary for producing efficient surrogates, enabling substantial computa-tional savings. Third, we build a benchmarking framework that treats the solver as a first-class component, allowing systematic investigation of what works across 46 PDE configurations, mul-tiple architectures, and diverse training strategies. We restrict ourselves to semi-linear PDEs on periodic, uniform Cartesian grids, a simplification that enables the Fourier-spectral analysis central to our arguments while still covering a rich set of representative dynamics. Figure 1.2 visually summarizes the connection between the pieces of research that make up this cumulative dissertation. 

# 1.4 Outline 

Chapter 2 reviews numerical methods for solving time-dependent PDEs on uniform Cartesian grids, covering spatial and temporal discretization, the spectral error analysis that underpins much of the later argumentation, and iterative solvers relevant to differentiable physics. The chapter also presents the JAX-based solver implementations (Exponax, Picardax, Chaotax) developed as part of this thesis. Chapter 3 introduces neural emulator architectures and draws structural parallels with numerical methods: convolutional networks and finite difference stencils, Fourier Neural Operators and spectral methods, UNets and multigrid. We describe the data-driven training pipeline for autoregressive emulators and discuss different aspects of generalization. Chapter 4 is the synthesis chapter. We identify five roles that numerical solvers play in the emulator pipeline, then use this framework to explain three phenomena: emulator superiority (proven via Fourier-spectral analysis), computational savings from progressive solver refinement (PRDP), and the benchmarking implications that follow from both. Chapter 5 describes the APEBench benchmarking suite, its software ecosystem, and systematic experimental findings. Chapter 6 

31. Introduction 

distills cross-cutting findings, discusses limitations and open questions, and outlines future directions, including foundation models, compute-optimal data generation, and the extension to unstructured meshes. Chapter 7 provides a concise summary of each publication. The full papers are reproduced in the Appendix.

# Chapter 2 

# Foundations of Numerical Simulation 

We want to solve time-dependent partial differential equations (PDEs) of the form 

∂u ∂t = Lu + N (u), (2.1) in which L and N (·) are linear and nonlinear differential operators, respectively. The nonlinear operator can be inhomogeneous , i.e., it can include a forcing term. This equation is solved for the solution function u(t, x). This function represents a field on the D-dimensional domain Ω ⊆ RD

for a time t ∈ [0 , T ). Typically, the domain is bounded. Then, we additionally supply boundary conditions on ∂Ω. For the time axis, one has to prescribe an initial condition , i.e., the value of 

u(t = 0 , x).An example is the one-dimensional Burgers equation, 

∂u ∂t + 12

∂u 2

∂x = ν∆u. (2.2) Here, the linear operator is the Laplace operator scaled by the diffusivity ν, i.e., L = ν∆. The nonlinear operator is the negated convection operator in conservative form, i.e., N (u) = − 12 

> ∂u 2
> ∂x

.We refer to the combination of a PDE, its constitutive parameters (e.g., the diffusivity ν), and boundary conditions as a PDE system P (also called an initial-boundary value problem ). A PDE system defines a continuous problem, but does not prescribe how it is solved on a computer. Choosing a numerical method M (e.g., finite differences with Crank-Nicolson time stepping), a spatial resolution N , and for time-dependent problems a time step ∆t, yields a discrete solver 

Ph (the subscript h and variations of it are used to indicate that this is a discrete approximation). This distinction matters because multiple solvers Ph, PH , . . . can approximate the same system P

at different fidelities , i.e., at different accuracies or with different properties. 

# 2.1 Numerical Methods for Time-Dependent PDEs 

The goal of simulation is to approximate the solution field u(t, x) on a computer. For this section, we will focus on time-dependent problems. However, parts of it are also relevant for stationary PDEs. A popular strategy is to follow the method-of-lines discretization (Figure 2.1) by finding a discrete representation of the function in the domain Ω and then integrate the discrete state uh in time. On structured grids, it is common to represent the state using higher-order tensors, e.g., in two dimensions uh ∈ RC×Nx×Ny 1. This representation is similar to images with C channels (for a physical field, these channels represent the vector components). Note that uh must not necessarily correspond to collocated grid values, but can also be coefficients of a collection of basis functions, as described in Section 2.1.1. For the remainder of this thesis, we will only consider uniform Cartesian grids that discretize the scaled unit cube Ω = (0 , L )D with N degrees of freedom per dimension. As such, a state in one, two, and three dimensions has two, three, and four axes, respectively (due to the leading channel

2.1.1 Spatial Discretization 

Spatial discretization approaches are characterized by (1) how they represent the domain, (2) how they approximate spatial derivatives, and (3) how they incorporate boundary conditions. For this thesis, we consider three (simplified) types of boundary conditions: 1. Periodic boundaries: u(t, x) xi=0 = u(t, x) xi=L

2. Homogeneous Dirichlet boundaries: u(t, x) xi∈{ 0,L } = 0 

3. Homogeneous Neumann boundaries: ∂u ∂x i (t, x) xi∈{ 0,L }

= 0 

Under periodic boundaries, we consider one end of the domain a degree of freedom. In 1D, this yields the grid xperiodic  

> h

= [0 , ∆x, 2∆ x, . . . , (N − 1)∆ x] with ∆x = L/N . For Dirichlet and Neumann boundaries, the degrees of freedom only consider the interior nodes. Hence, we would get xdirichlet/neumann  

> h

= [∆ x, 2∆ x, . . . , N ∆x] with ∆x = 1 /(N + 1) . Higher dimensional grids are built using tensor products. 

62. Foundations of Numerical Simulation 

Below, we review two popular approaches for approximating spatial derivatives on these grids: finite differences and Fourier spectral methods. See Figure 2.2 for a visual comparison of the two approaches. 

Finite Difference Methods 

Finite differences approximate derivatives using a Taylor series expansion (LeVeque, 2007). Com-putationally they can be expressed by a cross-correlation 2 “⋆” with a filter dh. For example, to approximate the second derivative, we could use the second-order approximation 

 ∂2u∂x 2



> h

= 1(∆ x)2 [1 , −2, 1] ⋆ uh + O  (∆ x)2 , (2.3) which produces a spatial discretization error that decays polynomially quadratically with ∆x. Note that this cross-correlation must be understood to operate only on the spatial axis corresponding to spatial dimension we are taking the derivative with respect to. All other axes are broadcasted. There are different filters, also called stencils, for different orders of derivatives with different orders of approximations (LeVeque, 2007). Vector calculus operations like gradients or divergence can be built by composing axis-wise derivatives. The approximation to the nonlinear differen-tial operator Nh(·) is straightforwardly implemented by combining derivatives and point-wise nonlinear evaluations. Importantly, the cross-correlation (convolution) must respect the boundary conditions. This is achieved by using "SAME" padding . The padding mode has to match the boundary type. For periodic boundaries, we can use circular padding. The homogeneous Dirichlet and Neumann boundaries can be implemented by padding with constant zeros and reflect mode, respectively. 

Spectral Methods 

Spectral methods use global Ansatz functions and apply transformations from collocated states 

uh into a coefficient space ˆuh in which derivative operations can be evaluated efficiently. In this thesis, we only consider Fourier-spectral methods that exclusively work with periodic boundary conditions (Boyd, 2001). Let Fh be the D-dimensional real-valued fast Fourier transform (in NumPy (Harris et al., 2020), this corresponds to np.fft.rfftn ). Without loss of generality, in 3D, this transforms our real-valued state tensor uh ∈ RC×N ×N ×N into a complex-valued coefficient tensor ˆuh ∈ CC×N ×N ×(N// 2+1) with N// 2 using integer division . Note that the real-valued Fourier transform approximately halved the trailing axis. Effectively, ˆuh still occupies approximately the same memory since it is complex-valued. Moreover, let us denote by F−1 

> h

the real-valued inverse fast Fourier transform. The entries in ˆuh correspond to coefficients of specific modes to which we can associate a wavenumber grid. For the right-most axis (that was halved by the real-valued transform), we have ˜kh = [0 , 1, 2, . . . , N// 2] (which has N// 2 + 1 entries). For all other axes, we have kh =[−N// 2, . . . , −2, −1, 0, 1, 2, . . . , N// 2 − 1] with the full N entries. The complete wavenumber grid is built as a tensor product similarly to how the spatial grid can be constructed. Then, we can take the d-th derivative in Fourier space simply by elementwise multiplying the coefficient state ˆuh by  i 2πL kh

◦d (the (·)◦d shall denote elementwise exponentiation). This allows for a spectral differentiation when employing forward and reverse transformations, e.g.,

The nonlinear part is then integrated using a suitable quadrature rule, which in the case of ETDRK1 is a simple forward Euler step. 

> 92. Foundations of Numerical Simulation

Higher-order ETDRK methods, that use more sophisticated quadrature rules, tend to achieve better accuracy (Montanelli and Bootland, 2020). For most practical cases, evaluating the matrix exponential e∆tLh is not feasible. However, for a Fourier-spectral discretization, this exponential is evaluated mode-wise, which is remarkably cheap. This builds the basis for the Exponax solvers (see Section 2.4.2) that underpins APEBench (Chapter 5). 

# 2.2 Implicit Integrators, Stationary Problems and Iterative Solves 

Implicit time integration requires solving linear or nonlinear systems of equations. The same algorithmic machinery also applies to stationary (steady-state) PDEs of the form 

Lu + N (u) = 0 . (2.12) A canonical example is the Poisson equation ∆u = −f (corresponding to L = ∆ and N (u) = f ). This reveals a natural connection between implicit time integration and stationary solvers: as the time step ∆t → ∞ , any implicit time integrator reduces to a solver for the stationary problem. Conversely, stationary solvers can be viewed as time integrators that converge in a single, infinitely large step. In both settings, the task reduces to finding a state u∗ 

> h

at which a residual operator Rh vanishes: 

Rh(u∗

> h

) = 0. The concrete form of Rh depends on the problem. For stationary PDEs, it encodes the discretized PDE constraint. In the case of implicit time integration, it depends on the particular scheme and typically involves the previous state. In the purely linear case, the residual reduces to 

Rh(uh) = Ahuh − bh.While direct methods exist for certain problems, most practical scenarios require iterative approaches . Let Φh(·) be an iteration operator that produces a sequence of states u(k+1)  

> h

=Φh

> h

starting from an initial guess u(0)  

> h

. We use superscript parentheses (k) to denote iterates and superscript brackets [n] for time steps. In the case of implicit time integration, these are nested: u[n+1] ,(k) 

> h

denotes the k-th iterate when resolving time step n + 1 .Ideally, the iterates converge to the solution u∗

> h

. Convergence can be measured by the (relative) 

optimality error u(k) 

> h

> h

∥. Since the exact solution is generally unavailable, a practical proxy is the (relative) residual error Rh(u(k) 

> h

> h

) . The iteration terminates when the relative residual drops below a prescribed tolerance ϵ (typically ϵ = 10 −5 for single-precision arithmetic) or a maximum number of iterations K is reached. We denote by Kϵ the number of iterations required to achieve tolerance ϵ.

2.2.1 Linear Solvers 

Linear systems Ahuh = bh arise when using implicit time integration on linear PDEs (i.e., 

N (·) = 0 ), when resolving linear stationary problems (such as the Poisson equation), or as a substep within nonlinear solvers. For high-rank tensor state representations, the matrix-vector product Ahuh must be understood as a linear map. Equivalently, one could reshape uh into a vector in RCN D

with Ah ∈ RCN D ×CN D

. In most cases, Ah is sparse , with only O(CN D)

non-zero entries out of (CN D)2.Oftentimes, there are assembly routines Λ( ·) and β(·) that build system matrix and right-hand side. For example, for a Poisson equation, β would build the right-hand side bh out of the forcing term f . For time stepping, the previous state might affect how matrix and right-hand side are built. More details on their specifics can be found in the appendix of the PRDP publication (see Chapter B). 

> 10 2. Foundations of Numerical Simulation

Given Ahuh = bh, several families of solvers exist, each with different trade-offs in computa-tional cost, convergence behavior, and applicability. 

Direct solvers employ matrix decompositions (e.g., LU or Cholesky) combined with forward and backward substitution (Golub and Van Loan, 2013). They are best suited for small or moderately sized, oftentimes dense, problems. Although, sparse variants do also exist. 

Relaxation methods use additive splittings of Ah and preferentially damp high-frequency error components. Their convergence is exponentially linear, with a rate governed by the spectral radius of the iteration matrix. Examples include Jacobi, Gauss-Seidel, and SOR methods (Saad, 2003). They are frequently used as smoothers within multigrid methods. 

Krylov methods construct iterates in expanding Krylov subspaces (Saad, 2003). Preconditioners are typically employed to accelerate convergence. 

Multigrid methods exploit the smoothing property of relaxation methods by operating across a hierarchy of resolutions: either geometrically via coarser meshes or algebraically via projected operators. They can achieve optimal O(N D) complexity for elliptic problems such as the Poisson equation, where information must propagate across the entire domain (Briggs et al., 2000). For linear systems with non-singular Ah, the solution u∗ 

> h

is unique and any initial guess u(0) 

> h

converges to the same result. In practice, one therefore typically initializes with u(0)  

> h

= 0. A common strategy is to combine solver paradigms. For instance, a Krylov method preconditioned by multigrid (which itself employs relaxation sweeps) or by a sparse incomplete factorization. This hybridization of complementary solver paradigms foreshadows the neural-hybrid methods discussed in Section 3.2.

2.2.3 Differentiating Through Iterative Solvers 

Explicit time integrators (Section 2.1.2) compose differentiable operations. Hence, automatic differentiation engines like JAX or PyTorch can propagate gradients directly. For iterative solvers, the situation is more nuanced. One option is to unroll the computation graph of K iterations of 

Φh. Given contractivity and smoothness of the iterator, the Jacobian estimate converges alongside the primal iterates (Gilbert, 1992). However, from a practical perspective, it is often preferable to differentiate over the converged solve rather than through the individual iterations (Blondel et al., 2022; Blondel and Roulet, 2024). This is particularly important when the solver is provided by a third-party library without native autodiff support. For a nonlinear solve, let Rh(uh; g) !

= 0 be the optimality condition, parameterized by g (e.g., the previous time step u[n] 

> h

). Implicit differentiation of this condition yields the Jacobian 

∂uh

∂g = − [∂0Rh(uh; g)] −1 ∂1Rh(uh; g), (2.16) which requires solving a linear solve, regardless of how many iterations the forward solve took. This can be used to implement custom forward- or reverse-mode differentiation rules 3. For more details, we refer to the Appendix of the PRDP publication in Chapter B. 

# 2.3 Spectral Error Analysis 

The consistency error of the finite difference derivative approximation in Equation (2.3) used 

O((∆ x)2) to bound the value. Due to spatial mixing, finding a closed-form expression of the approximation error is challenging. Fortunately, if we limit ourselves to periodic boundary conditions, we can use a Fourier-based analysis. By the convolution theorem (Boyd, 2001), the kernel dh acts element-wise in Fourier space, leading to mode-wise error analysis. This allows not only to investigate the spatial discretization error but also the mode-wise error if we     

> 3Interactive reference tables for explicit and implicit autodiff primitive rules are available online. See https: //fkoehler.site/autodiff-table/ and https://fkoehler.site/implicit-autodiff-table/ .

12 2. Foundations of Numerical Simulation 

combine it with a temporal integration scheme. As such, we can precisely identify what errors the evolution operator Ph introduces, and how constitutive parameters (like diffusivity ν), numerical scheme M, and discretization choces N and ∆t affect them. Moreover, we can use exact spectral differentiation with exact exponential time differnece to obtain a reference for what each mode multiplier should be. It is this analytical tractability that motivates the restriction to periodic, uniform Cartesian grids. For simplicity, we will here only consider the linear one-dimensional diffusion equation equation 

∂tu = ν∆u on a periodic domain. This discussion is extended in the emulator superiority paper of Chapter C. Let ϕ = m/N ∈ [0 , 1/2] denote the relative wavenumber (associated with the mode index m) and γ2 = 2 νN 2∆t/L 2 the dimensionless diffusion parameter (a CFL-like number). Each time-stepping scheme maps a Fourier mode ˆu[n] 

> m

to ˆu[n+1]  

> m

by multiplying with the multiplier (amplification factor) relevant for index m. Below we list the multipliers for three different schemes. First, we find the exact or analytical multiplier 4 ˆαϕ

ˆαϕ = exp 



− γ2

2 (2 πϕ )2

. (2.17) The FTCS scheme (forward Euler in time, central differences in space) yields 

ˆeϕ = 1 + γ2 (cos(2 πϕ ) − 1) , (2.18) which is stable only for γ2 ≤ 1. The implicit BTCS scheme (backward Euler in time, central differences in space) gives 

ˆιϕ = 11 − γ2 (cos(2 πϕ ) − 1) , (2.19) which is unconditionally stable. Comparing ˆeϕ and ˆιϕ against the analytical multiplier ˆαϕ reveals a characteristic pattern. The BTCS scheme always under -attenuates, i.e., it diffuses less than the analytical solution. Both of its error sources (the implicit Euler step and the central difference stencil) act in the same direction. For the FTCS scheme, the two sources compete: the forward Euler over -attenuates, while the central difference stencil under-attenuates. Hence, the net sign depends on γ2 and the considered mode ϕ. Both schemes are exact at ϕ = 0 (the mean mode) and deviate most strongly near the Nyquist frequency ϕ = 1 /2. The discrepancy between the numerical and analytical multipliers constitutes the discretization error per mode. This mode-wise perspective will be central to understanding the emulator superiority phenomenon in Section 4.2, where we extend the same analysis to learned emulators and show that their inductive biases can implicitly correct for these structured numerical errors (see Figure 4.2 for a combined visualization of the multipliers across a range of ϕ values). 

# 2.4 Modern Implementations: Solvers in Machine Learning Frame-works 

Classically, numerical solvers were implemented in fast and compiled languages like Fortran or C++. The advent of multicore CPUs and multi-node clusters required extending these codes with parallelization frameworks like OpenMP or MPI, giving rise to well-established suites such as Eigen (Guennebaud, Jacob, et al., 2010), PETSc (Balay et al., 2026), or OpenFOAM (Weller et al., 1998). Meanwhile, most deep learning research is conducted in Python using machine learning frameworks like PyTorch (Paszke et al., 2019) or JAX (Bradbury et al., 2018). These  

> 4Interestingly, since Fourier-spectral ETDRK methods can solve (bandlimited) linear PDEs on periodic grids exactly ,the analytical multiplier in Equation (2.17) is precisely the one realized by the Exponax solver of Section 2.4.2.
> 13

2. Foundations of Numerical Simulation 

frameworks build on the tradition of MATLAB and NumPy: wrapping efficient linear algebra routines in a high-level interface. Additionally, they abstract hardware execution across CPUs and accelerators like GPUs, and provide automatic differentiation engines that form the core pillar of gradient-based learning (Baydin et al., 2018). Many also ship optimizing compilers (e.g., XLA for JAX) that produce efficient fused kernels for diverse devices. A natural approach to data generation for physics-based deep learning is to employ proven solvers, write datasets to disk, and load them in the machine learning framework of choice. However, this gives rise to a two-language problem : one cannot query a solver on-the-fly, nor differentiate through it to build neural-hybrid surrogates. 5

An alternative is to reimplement solvers directly within machine learning frameworks or modern languages like Julia (Bezanson et al., 2017). This approach unlocks several benefits, including backend-agnostic execution across CPUs, GPUs, and other accelerators with minimal boilerplate. Moreover, native autodiff compatibility enables sensitivity analysis, design optimization, chaos studies (e.g., Lyapunov exponents), neural-hybrid architectures, and using physics Jacobians as regularizers. Such reimplementations also demonstrate that classical solvers can be surprisingly competitive on modern accelerators (McGreivy and Hakim, 2024). Challenges remain, however. Wrapping existing third-party solvers (and making them dif-ferentiable) is nontrivial, and certain algorithms (like multigrid on unstructured grids) do not map straightforwardly to the tensor-style programming models of current ML frameworks. The broader ecosystem of established simulation codes (FEniCS (Logg et al., 2012), deal.II (Arndt et al., 2021), SU2 (Economon et al., 2016), Dedalus (Burns et al., 2020), among others) represents decades of development that cannot easily be replicated. Still, there is a growing list of solvers in modern ML frameworks. Most prominently, this includes PhiFlow (Holl and Thuerey, 2024) that initially translated computer graphics fluid simulation (Stam, 1999) techniques to TensorFlow, and then generalized to a wider array of physics phenomena and simulation techniques. Kochkov et al. (2021) introduced jax-cfd , which has later been ported to spectral methos by Dresdner et al. (2023). Bezgin et al. (2023) contributed  

> JAX-Fluids

which is a higher-order finite volume solver that has been shown to scale to 1024 TPU cores (Bezgin et al., 2025). The work of Xue et al. (2023) with JAX-FEM is a first approach to porting the finite element machinery to JAX. PICT (Franz et al., 2025) is a GPU-accelerated PISO solver with a direct and differentiable interface to PyTorch. Winchenbach and Thuerey (2026) presented a differentiable SPH-simulator in PyTorch. 

2.4.1 Why JAX? 

JAX’s functional programming model is a natural fit for numerical time steppers Ph, which are inherently stateless maps from one state to the next. Its vmap transformation enables automatic vectorization: one writes a solver for a single instance and batches it over thousands of initial conditions or parameter configurations with a single line of code. Similarly, lax.scan provides a clean abstraction for time-stepping loops. JAX’s composable higher-order derivatives make Hessians straightforward, which is valuable for PDE-constrained optimization and sensitivity analysis. Its explicit handling of pseudo-random number generation ensures bitwise reproducibil-ity across runs, an important property for scientific computing. From a performance perspective, JAX’s XLA compiler fuses kernel operations automatically, producing efficient code for CPUs, 

> 5Efforts exist to bridge this gap. For example, Enzyme is an autodiff engine operating on the LLVM intermediate representation, enabling programs to exchange gradient information even across different frontend languages (Moses and Churavy, 2020). Aehle et al. (2022) presents an effort to apply automatic differentiation to compiled programs. Tesseract provides a standardized interface for composing differentiable components across frameworks (Häfner and Lavin, 2025). In a classical multi-physics setting, preCICE is a coupling library for simulations across different codes and languages (Chourdakis et al., 2022).

14 2. Foundations of Numerical Simulation 

GPUs, and TPUs without manual optimization. 

2.4.2 Exponax: Differentiable Spectral Solvers 

Exponax is a JAX library implementing ETDRK solvers ( Section 2.1.2) for semi-linear PDEs on periodic domains. By relying exclusively on Fourier spectral methods for spatial discretization, it maps efficiently to GPUs and TPUs by using highly optimized FFT routines. When using substeps, it can also handle moderately nonlinear systems such as the Navier-Stokes equations at low-to-medium Reynolds numbers. For more details, see the appendix of the APEBench in Chapter A. The key practical advantage is speed. Together with JAX’s vmap for automatic vectorization over initial conditions, Exponax can generate large datasets in seconds on modern accelerators. Since no implicit solves are involved, automatic differentiation is unambiguous, i.e., there are no iterative convergence tolerances that could affect gradient quality (see Sections 2.2.3 and 4.3.2). Exponax is heavily inspired by the Chebfun library (Driscoll et al., 2014), which is a reliable implementation of (Fourier-)spectral ETDRK methods in MATLAB. Chebfun served as an early data generator for works such as the original PINN (Raissi et al., 2019) and FNO (Li et al., 2021) publications. 

2.4.3 Picardax: Differentiable Finite Difference Solvers 

Picardax implements time-implicit solvers in JAX (Koehler, 2026b). It supports θ-method time stepping, encompassing explicit Euler ( θ = 0 ), implicit Euler ( θ = 1 ), and Crank-Nicolson (θ = 1 /2) (Turek, 1999). Using a general linearized form, the library automatically extracts the system matrix assembly Λ and right-hand side assembly β from the PDE discretization, then dispatches to a fixed-point (Picard) iterator with configurable convergence criteria. It is built around the idea of differentiable implicit time integrators (see Section 2.2.3). Beyond its role as a solver, Picardax serves as a research tool for studying the interplay between iterative solver convergence and gradient quality. This capability directly underpins the PRDP work (full publication in Chapter B): by controlling the number of solver iterations, one can investigate how under-converged solves affect the training signal. Moreover, Picardax enables the study of selective gradient cuts. For instance, deactivating backpropagation through the assembly routine Λ in a Picard-style iteration, a practice common in CFD applications (List et al., 2022). In this sense, Picardax connects PDE simulation to the broader bilevel optimization literature. 

2.4.4 Chaotax: Inferring Dynamical System Properties with Autodiff in JAX 

The Lyapunov exponent λ quantifies the exponential error divergence of a dynamical system via 

u[n] 

> h

− u[n],ref h ≈ eλn ∆t u[0]  

> h

− u[0] ,ref h . (2.20) If λ is positive, the dynamical system (like in the case of the integration of a time-dependent PDE) is considered chaotic (Gilpin, 2021). As such, time integration via Ph is sensitive to small changes in the initial condition. Small errors inevitably grow exponentially. We can approximate the largest Lyapunov exponent numerically by integrating the tangent-linear system for a perturbation alongside the integration of the primal system. The primal system is integrated by repeatedly calling Ph. We can use forward-mode automatic differentiation for the Jacobian-vector-product effect of JPh (Balcerzak and Leo Kingston, 2025). Similarly, one can obtain the full Lyapunov spectrum {λ1, λ 2, ..., } of Ph with an automatic differentiation approach (Balcerzak and Leo Kingston, 2025; Benettin et al., 1980). It requires 

> 15 2. Foundations of Numerical Simulation

integrating a collection of tangent linear systems next to the primal one (i.e., thereby combining both the automatic differentiation and automatic vectorization of JAX). A full spectrum is helpful to categorize dynamics into chaotic, dissipative, or hyperbolic and to find intrinsic properties of chaotic attractors like the Kaplan-Yorke dimension (Gilpin, 2021). Since the solvers Exponax and Picardax are implemented in JAX, using autodiff to evaluate corresponding Lyapunov properties is straightforward. With Chaotax we provide a lightweight and efficient implementation (Koehler, 2026a). Beyond dynamics categorization, Lyapunov ex-ponents inform meaningful evaluation horizons for autoregressive emulators, as discussed in Section 4.4 and in Mikhaeil et al. (2022).

# Chapter 3 

# Neural Emulators for PDEs 

In the previous chapter, we abstracted the solution process of a partial differential equation using the evolution operator Ph (either as a time stepper or the solver to a stationary problem). This chapter considers the neural emulator fθ that is trained to mimic the simulator’s behavior, i.e., 

fθ ≈ P h. This requires constructing a functional form, i.e., a neural architecture, and a training procedure to select “optimal” θ.Crucially, we only consider neural surrogates which, similar to Ph, have all the modeling and discretization choices baked into them . In other words, they operate for one PDE, under one parameterization, a single type of boundary conditions, a fixed resolution N , and, for temporal problems, a fixed ∆t. Many works try to be more general and, e.g., allow the model to be 

conditioned on constitutive parameters or identify them based on a history input. This can be generalized to allow for a model to solve many systems {Pi}Pi=1 and smoothly interpolate between them (Herde et al., 2024). We will discuss this more in Section 6.3.1. By design, we restrict ourselves to full-field surrogates meant to replace the numerical solver 

Ph entirely. Several orthogonal directions exist. Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) (Raissi et al., 2019) learn the solution function u(t, x) of Equation (2.1) directly by optimizing residual-based losses and can work without supervision. Machine learning components can also replace classical heuristic elements in simulation pipelines, such as flux limiters (Discacciati et al., 2020). 

# 3.1 Neural Architectures as Learned Discretizations 

In this section, we will introduce the most popular architectures for neural emulators on structured Cartesian grids. All of them first lift the input state uh in the channel axis, e.g., in 1D uh ∈

RC×N 7 → u[l=0]  

> h

∈ RClifted ×N . Afterward, this state is processed. Ultimately, it is projected back to the output shape. In our case, we often have that the output has the same number of channels as the input since the models operate autoregressively. In Figure 3.1, we visually compare how the different architectures process spatial dependencies. 

3.1.1 Convolutional Networks and Finite Difference Stencils 

A convolutional layer applies a learned filter via cross-correlation. This is the same operation used to approximate spatial derivatives in finite difference methods (Section 2.1.1). In fact, a single convolutional layer with kernel size K = 3 , no bias, and no nonlinearity is exactly a finite difference stencil with learned coefficients (Long et al., 2018). Convolutional Networks (ConvNets or CNNs) apply a sequence of convolutional layers on the lifted representation (LeCun et al., 1989). What a convolutional network adds beyond a single stencil is depth, channel mixing, and nonlinearity. Each layer has O(C2

> lifted

KD) parameters and O(C2

> lifted

KDN D) compute. Channel mixing enables the network to couple multiple physical quantities (e.g., velocity components and pressure), much as a system of PDEs couples multiple fields. Pointwise nonlinearities after each layer allow the network to represent nonlinear operators. Stacking L layers with kernel size 

K = 3 yields a receptive field of L grid points per direction per dimension. Residual connections (He et al., 2016a) and pre-layer normalization (He et al., 2016b) are essential for training deep

Each panel highlights which input positions contribute to a single output position (dark blue) in one layer or block; shading intensity indicates contribution strength. Panels are ordered by locality of the receptive field: (a) ConvNet ( ≈ finite differences) uses a fixed local stencil; (b) SWIN attends within shifted windows; (c) Dilated ResNet grows the receptive field through dilation; (d) UNet ( ≈ multigrid) processes a hierarchy of resolutions with skip connections; (e) ViT applies global attention between patches; (f) FNO ( ≈ spectral methods) mixes all positions through the Fourier domain; (g) Transformer uses full self-attention over every position. 

networks and can be interpreted as a learned identity-plus-perturbation structure, echoing the

3.1.2 Fourier Neural Operators and Spectral Methods 

Where convolutional networks mirror finite difference stencils, Fourier Neural Operators (FNOs) mirror (Fourier) spectral methods. Recall that spectral differentiation amounts to elementwise multiplication by wavenumber-dependent coefficients in Fourier space (Equation (2.4)). An FNO layer does the same, but with learned complex multipliers: the input is transformed via the FFT, multiplied mode-wise by a learnable weight tensor, and transformed back (Li et al., 2021). Where the spectral method prescribes the multipliers from the PDE, the FNO learns them from data. In 1D on a discrete representation with N degrees of freedom, a full parameterization would require N// 2 + 1 complex multipliers per channel pair, which is substantially more parameters than a spatial convolution with kernel size K ≪ N . In practice, FNOs retain only the lowest 

M ≈ 12 ∼ 20 modes and set higher-mode multipliers to zero. This acts as an inherent low-pass filter. Li et al. (2021) argue that residual connections and stacking multiple Fourier layers with pointwise nonlinearities can recover some higher-frequency content, but the low-pass character remains a defining trait. Channel mixing happens in Fourier space, hence, each layer has O(C2

> lifted

M D) parameters. The compute per layer is O(Clifted N D log N +C2

> lifted

M D), typically dominated by the FFT for multidimensional problems. A known limitation is that the Fourier basis assumes periodic boundary conditions. Despite this, FNOs are increasingly applied to non-periodic problems. Here, the mode truncation appears to reduce the impact of the Gibbs phenomenon (Boyd, 2001). Alternative strategies include zero-padding the domain to approximate a periodic extension 1. Architectures like GINO address unstructured domains by first mapping to a uniform grid and then applying FNO processing (Li et al., 2023b).  

> 1See, e.g., the NVIDIA Neural Operator library at https://github.com/NVIDIA/neuraloperator .

18 3. Neural Emulators for PDEs 

Several extensions reduce the cost or increase the flexibility of FNOs. Factorized FNOs (Tran et al., 2023) apply the FFT independently per spatial dimension. Other approaches parameterize the multipliers as continuous functions of the mode index (Xiao et al., 2024). 

3.1.3 UNets as Multigrid Methods 

To overcome the slow growth of receptive field in feedforward ConvNets, Ronneberger et al. (2015) used convolutions on a hierarchy of grid resolutions with appropriate downsampling and upsampling operations. Given a kernel size of 3, a single convolution at hierarchy level l covers 2l

grid points per direction at the original resolution. For example, when coarsening the resolution 

l = 4 times, one convolution already spans 16 grid points. In contrast, a flat ConvNet would need 

16 layers to achieve this. This hierarchical structure closely resembles geometric multigrid (Section 2.2.1). In multigrid, relaxation sweeps smooth the error on the current grid, a restriction operator projects the residual to a coarser grid where long-range components are corrected cheaply, and a prolongation oper-ator interpolates the correction back (Briggs et al., 2000). The UNet follows the same pattern: convolution blocks at each level act as learned smoothers, downsampling as learned restriction, upsampling as learned prolongation, and skip connections carry fine-scale information across the hierarchy much like the coarse-grid correction in multigrid. In practice, several choices exist for the downsampling and upsampling operations. The “modern UNet” for PDE emulation (Gupta and Brandstetter, 2023) replaces the max-pooling of the original architecture with strided convolutions for downsampling. An increasingly popular alternative is PixelUnshuffle (space-to-depth) for downsampling and PixelShuffle (depth-to-space) for upsampling (Shi et al., 2016): each r × r spatial patch is rearranged into r2 channels (or vice versa), followed by a learned linear projection. An alternative modernization replaces the residual blocks with ConvNeXt blocks (Liu et al., 2022), which separate spatial and channel mixing via depthwise convolutions with large kernels and use an inverted bottleneck design. This ConvNeXt-based UNet emerged as the strongest single baseline across the diverse PDE problems in The Well benchmark (Ohana et al., 2024). In principle, the hierarchical structure is not limited to convolutional processing. Recent work has incorporated Fourier layers or attention mechanisms into the UNet hierarchy (Herde et al., 2024; Holzschuh et al., 2025), just as multigrid can employ different smoothers at different levels. 

3.1.4 Transformer-based Architecture 

The previous architectures all encode spatial structure explicitly: convolutions enforce locality, Fourier layers enforce global periodicity, and UNets enforce a resolution hierarchy. Attention-based mechanisms take a different approach. Originally developed for sequence modelling in natural language processing (Bahdanau et al., 2015; Vaswani et al., 2017), they provide a nonlocal 

mechanism to mix in “space” (in a language modelling task, this refers to the tokens of the sequence). As such, they have been adapted to neural surrogates to replace the spatial or spectral convolution-based processing. A vanilla transformer architecture can be ported to work on uniform Cartesian states in 1D, 2D, or 3D by removing the causal attention mask and employing appropriate positional embeddings using tensor-product sinusoidal encodings (Vaswani et al., 2017) or the more modern RoPE (Su et al., 2024). Unfortunately, the compute requirements of attention scale quadratically in the number of tokens, i.e., in a vanilla approach it scales quadratically with the total number of degrees of freedom, giving O(N 2D).Several strategies reduce this cost. Vision Transformers (ViTs) partition the grid into non-overlapping patches and treat each patch as a single token, so that self-attention operates over 

O(( N/p )D) tokens instead of N D (Dosovitskiy et al., 2021). Shifted-window (SWIN) attention 

> 19 3. Neural Emulators for PDEs

restricts attention to local windows and shifts the window boundaries between layers, so that tokens at window edges interact in alternating layers, giving the architecture global connectivity over depth (Liu et al., 2021). Factorization of attention across spatial dimensions further reduces cost (Li et al., 2023a; McCabe et al., 2024). Paischer et al. (2025) demonstrated that shifted-window attention can scale to five spatial dimensions. Since attention does not assume any spatial structure, transformers extend naturally to un-structured representations with appropriate positional embeddings (Alkin et al., 2025; 2024; Wu et al., 2024; Zhdanov et al., 2025), a direction we discuss further in Section 6.3.3. Compared to convolutions or Fourier layers, transformers introduce very little physical induc-tive bias. They do not bake in locality, periodicity, or translation equivariance. Modern variants like SWIN or hierarchical designs reintroduce some of these priors, but the vanilla form is almost entirely domain-agnostic. Despite this, there is growing momentum behind transformer-based surrogates as the architecture of choice for foundation models in scientific computing (Ashton et al., 2025), echoing observations from language modelling (Brown et al., 2020). Whether physical inductive biases for neural surrogates remain valuable at sufficient scale, or whether they are subsumed by sheer model capacity and data diversity, is an open question. 

3.1.5 Other Types of Architectures 

Another popular mechanism to increase the receptive field is to dilate the convolutional kernels (Stachenfeld et al., 2022; Yu and Koltun, 2016), effectively inserting zeros between the kernel values. With a dilation factor d, a kernel of size K spans d(K − 1) + 1 grid points. By stacking layers with exponentially growing dilation rates, one can cover the full domain in logarithmically many layers rather than linearly many. Neural surrogates can be outfitted with an internal iteration. Oftentimes, this comes in the form of diffusion models or flow matching approaches (Holderrieth and Erives, 2026). This seems natural when solving inherently stiff problems like directly predicting into the turbulent manifold (Lienen et al., 2024; Lino et al., 2025). Kohl et al. (2026) investigated whether autoregressive diffusion models can improve long-term accuracy and stability while Lippe et al. (2023) argue that a diffusion-based iterative refinement helps with high-frequency accuracy and potentially mitigates distribution shift. On unstructured meshes, Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) exchange messages along edges of the mesh graph (Pfaff et al., 2021; Sanchez-Gonzalez et al., 2020). Each message-passing step aggregates information from neighboring nodes. This is a local operation, much like a convolutional layer on a structured grid. Stacking multiple layers grows the receptive field analogously to stacking convolutions. The same idea of hierarchical processing from Section 3.1.3 has been adapted to graphs by introducing coarsened mesh levels (Cao et al., 2023; Grigorev et al., 2023). 

# 3.2 Neural-Hybrid Approaches 

Instead of fully replacing the numerical solver Ph by fθ, we can introduce a coarse simulator 

PH (note the capital H to indicate it being a coarser form of the fine simulator) and have a neural component cθ correct its output or interact with it. The appeal is that decades of expertise have gone into building numerical solvers (Chapter 2), and a hybrid approach can leverage that structure rather than re-learning it from scratch. At the same time, operating at the state-discrete level is more flexible than replacing a single algorithmic component like a flux limiter (Discacciati et al., 2020). In the simplest case this could be sequential, i.e., fθ = cθ ◦ P H (or fθ = PH ◦ cθ) (List et al., 

> 20

3. Neural Emulators for PDEs 

2022; Um et al., 2020) 2. More complex interactions are possible, e.g., by having the correction term interact with the solver’s internal state or by having the correction term interact with the solver’s numerics, e.g., a learned finite volume interpolation (Kochkov et al., 2021) or as a learned forcing term (Wei et al., 2025). For example, hybrid approaches have been used in building fast weather and climate emulators (Kochkov et al., 2024). Given certain learning setups (e.g., under an unrolled setting as we will discuss in Section 3.3.1), the combined component fθ is required to be output-input differentiable (not just with respect to the parameters θ). Consequently, this requires the coarse solver PH to be differentiable. This introduces practical challenges: differentiable solvers can be expensive to backpropagate through, and iterative solvers may not fully converge, raising questions about gradient quality. In Section 4.3, we show that full convergence of PH is in fact unnecessary during training and that this can be exploited for significant computational savings. 

# 3.3 Training and Testing of Autoregressive Neural Emulators 

3.3.1 Synthetic Data Generation and Training Configurations

(3.2) Here, ζ is typically the mean squared error, though relative losses like the relative L2 norm are also common, especially for multi-channel systems where physical quantities vary in magnitude. The emulator fθ is trained to match the solver Ph in expectation over pairs of consecutive states from a trajectory (Figure 3.2a). In practice, the expectation is approximated using a finite dataset and stochastic minibatches. Then, the optimization follows a standard deep learning pipeline with momentum-accelerated stochastic gradient descent (Prince, 2023), with Adam (Kingma and Ba, 2015) being the most common optimizer choice. Many variations of this one-step supervised setting exist. Most prominently, unrolled training optimizes over trajectory mismatch by applying fθ autoregressively for more than one step and comparing the entire predicted trajectory against the reference (Figure 3.2a, light purple gradient flow). This can improve long-term stability (Kohl et al., 2026), but requires backpropagation through the autoregressive chain. Additional modifications include weighting individual time steps differently in the loss, and curriculum strategies that gradually increase the unroll length during training (Grigorev et al., 2023). Figure 3.2.: Training and evaluation pipeline for autoregressive neural emulators. (a) Dur-ing training, a numerical solver Ph produces reference trajectories from initial conditions sampled from Ih. Similarly, adding small amounts of noise to the training inputs (Pfaff et al., 2021; Sanchez-Gonzalez et al., 2020) serves a comparable purpose by perturbing the inputs away from the clean solver trajectory. The temporal axis taxonomy of Figure 3.3 characterizes the emulator’s input-output structure along three dimensions: the history length H, skip C, and bundle size L. The baseline setting (H = C = L = 1 ) corresponds to a Markovian single-step emulator, mirroring one-step numerical methods like Euler or Runge-Kutta. Using a history H ≥ 2 is analogous to multi-step methods (e.g., Adams-Bashforth) and can capture higher-order temporal information. Interestingly, solid performance is often achieved with the simple Markovian setting, suggesting that the dynamics of many PDE systems are well approximated by single-step maps. This is consistent with the prevalence of one-step methods for first-order PDEs in numerical practice (Ruiz et al., 2025). 

3.3.2 Different Aspects of Generalization 

At test time, autoregressive emulators are evaluated by rolling out the model for multiple time steps and comparing against a reference trajectory (Figure 3.2b),

Temporal/Autoregressive generalization involves evaluating long-term accuracy and stabil-ity by testing for n > 1, i.e., for more autoregressive applications than during training. Exponential error growth e[n] usually signals instability.

Figure 3.3.: The (H, C, L ) temporal axis taxonomy. An autoregressive emulator fθ maps 

H input states to L output states, potentially skipping C−1 intermediate time levels. (a) The standard Markovian baseline ( H=C=L=1 ). (b) Using H≥2 past states as input enables higher-order temporal information, analogous to multi-step numerical methods. (c) A skip length C≥2 coarsens the effective time step; skipped time levels (hollow circles) are not predicted. (d) Temporal bundling ( L≥2) produces multiple future states simultaneously. 

State-space generalization examines performance on different initial conditions ˜Ih̸ = Ih or over extended time horizons beyond those used for training data generation, where the physics might enter a regime that is not well-represented by the training data. 

Metric generalization refers to evaluating the emulator using a different metric ˜ζ than the one used for training ζ. This allows for probing specific aspects of performance, such as accuracy in higher frequencies/modes. 

Solver generalization evaluates against a different numerical simulator ˜Ph̸ = Ph, where both solvers are consistent with the same underlying PDE (2.1) but employ different numerical implementations, varying in aspects such as scheme order or solver tolerance. Emulators can be tested for a single type of generalization or a combination of several. It is important to distinguish these axes clearly: for instance, autoregressive generalization and state-space generalization are often conflated in practice, since long rollouts naturally push the emulator into unseen regions of state space. The generalization box in Figure 3.2b summarizes these four axes. The emulator’s behavior across these axes can often be analyzed from a spectral perspective: similar to numerical methods (whose error behavior is discussed in Section 2.3), emulators may perform better or worse depending on the spectral composition of the state. Beyond the four axes above, one may additionally probe generalization over resolution, con-stitutive parameters, or even entirely different PDE systems. These are questions that become central in the context of foundation models (Section 6.3.1). 

23 Chapter 4 

# The Solver-Emulator Relationship 

This chapter sets out to explain three observations about neural emulators of PDEs that are difficult to reconcile with a simple data-driven view of surrogation. First, an emulator trained on trajectories from a given numerical solver can, at inference time, produce multi-step rollouts whose error against a higher-fidelity—in some cases analytical—reference is strictly smaller than that of the solver which generated its training data. Second, differentiable-physics training can reach the same final accuracy at a fraction of the solver iteration budget that asymptotic convergence analysis would demand. Third, two apparently identical benchmarks can yield opposing verdicts about the same emulator under assumptions that are rarely stated explicitly. We will argue that all three observations share a common cause. Chapter 2 and Chapter 3 introduced numerical solvers and neural emulators as separate paradigms. We already noted structural parallels between specific architectures and classical methods, e.g., ConvNets and finite differences (Section 3.1.1), Fourier Neural Operators and pseudo-spectral methods (Section 3.1.2), UNets and multigrid (Section 3.1.3). This is in line with a broader pattern documented in the literature (Alt et al., 2023; Brandstetter et al., 2022; McCabe et al., 2023; Toshev et al., 2023a). The entanglement, however, runs deeper than architecture. At the level of the training and evaluation 

pipeline (Section 3.3), a “solver” is not a single object but several: one generates the training data, a (possibly different) one serves as the reference against which the emulator is evaluated, a (possibly third) one may be embedded inside the training loop, and so on. Conflating these silently is what makes each of the three observations above appear puzzling. Section 4.1 formalizes this picture by identifying five distinct roles that numerical solvers occupy in the emulator pipeline and the fidelity relationships among them. Each subsequent section then pins one of the three observations to a specific role configuration: emulators exceeding their training source (Section 4.2), training-loop solvers that need not converge (Section 4.3), and the benchmarking consequences that follow from both (Section 4.4). 

# 4.1 The Multiple Roles of the Solver 

Recall from Chapter 2 that a single PDE system P does not have a unique numerical counterpart. The same governing equations can be discretized with different methods M, resolutions N , and time steps ∆t. Each choice yields a distinct solver Ph with its own error structure and other properties. We introduced the notion of fidelity (Chapter 2) to compare them. Two solvers Ph and 

PH approximating the same P need not be equally accurate and can be ordered along a spectrum of fidelity, made precise in the spectral sense of Section 2.3. What is specific to neural emulation is that the emulator pipeline silently involves multiple 

such discretizations at once, at potentially different fidelities, each performing a different job. We distinguish five roles (Figure 4.1): 1. Training data source. The solver whose trajectories Dh the network is trained on. Its numerical errors enter the emulator through the supervised loss. Its fidelity is essentially arbitrary: the emulator only sees what the dataset was generated with. 2. Evaluation reference. The solver against which the emulator is scored at test time; this is

Figure 4.1.: The multiple roles of numerical solvers in the neural emulator pipeline. A continuous PDE system P admits many valid discretizations Ph, PH , Ph′ , . . . obtained by choosing a numerical method M, resolution N , and time step ∆t. Each solver may fill one or more of the five roles shown, and different experimental setups activate different subsets (see Table 4.1). Solid arrows denote data flow into the emulator, double-headed arrows denote comparison, and the dashed arrow denotes conceptual influence. 

what “accuracy” actually measures. This raises the question how its fidelity being different from (1) affects the benchmarking of a neural emulator. 3. Hybrid or in-the-loop component. A differentiable solver called inside the training loop, either as part of a neural-hybrid architecture or as a physics-informed component through which gradients flow. Its fidelity can be chosen flexibly (often coarser than (1)) since its job is to work together with a neural component, not to produce reference trajectories. 4. Structural analogue. The classical numerical method that the architecture structurally resembles. Unlike the other roles, (4) does not participate computationally in the pipeline. It provides an inductive bias. 5. Baseline comparator. The solver against which the emulator is compared on the accuracy– speed trade-off. Its fidelity should match the claim being made: comparing against a very coarse solver benefits the emulator, against a very fine one penalizes it. Two observations about role (4) deserve emphasis, since it is qualitatively different from the others. First, it is conceptual rather than computational. No solver needs to exist in memory for role (4) to be filled. It is enough that the architecture is structurally analogous to one. Second, role (4) can be essentially vacant : for architectures with no clear classical counterpart (e.g., vanilla transformers are the canonical case) no numerical method supplies such a bias, and any plausibility prior must come from elsewhere. This can be the data distribution, the training objective, or explicit physical constraints. Both observations will reappear in the superiority analysis of Section 4.2. Not every experimental setup activates all five roles, and the fidelity relationships among the active ones can vary. In the default setup of training on trajectories and evaluating against held-out trajectories from the same solver, roles (1), (2), and (5) collapse onto a single solver, role (3) is absent, and role (4) is whatever the architecture provides. Superiority investigations (Section 4.2) deliberately break this by introducing a strictly higher-fidelity reference (2) distinct 

26 4. The Solver-Emulator Relationship 

from (1). PRDP (Section 4.3) introduces role (3) with intentionally low and varying fidelity. Finally, an unfair benchmark arises when role (2) is not separately available at all. This is the case for most fixed, published benchmarks such as PDEBench (Takamoto et al., 2022), where the data-generating solver is not exposed as a configurable component. As such, roles (1), (2), and (5) collapse opaquely into the dataset itself and there is no way to tell whether the emulator merely reproduces its training source’s errors. Table 4.1 summarises these configurations alongside a neural-hybrid variant and the symbolic conventions used in the rest of the chapter.

# 4.2 When Emulators exceed their Training Source 

The conventional assumption is that a neural emulator trained on data from a numerical solver can, at best, reproduce the accuracy of that solver. The emulator superiority result challenges this: under certain conditions, the emulator’s inductive biases perform implicit regularization that corrects for structured numerical artifacts in the training data. While this may not hold in all practical scenarios, it is a call to be cautious about where training data comes from and what one evaluates against. 

4.2.1 Spectral Error Analysis of Learned Emulators 

For simple linear PDEs, we can perform the same mode-wise analysis as in Section 2.3, but now for learned emulators. Consider an emulator with the functional form of the FTCS scheme in Equation (2.18) but with a free parameter θ replacing γ2,

ˆqϕ(θ) = 1 + θ (cos(2 πϕ ) − 1) . (4.1) This ansatz has the same inductive bias as the FTCS scheme. However, the parameter θ is not derived from first principles about the PDE and its constitutive parameter. Instead, it is fit from data by minimizing the squared error at a training mode ψ1. Depending on which solver generates the training data, the optimal θ∗ differs. If the training data comes from the FTCS scheme, the optimal fit recovers θ = γ2 exactly. The ansatz reproduces its training source. If the training data comes from the implicit BTCS of Equation (2.19) scheme, fitting at mode ψ yields 

θ∗ = γ2

As such, the emulator has exceeded its training source in certain frequency bands. In Figure 4.2, we plot a representative evaluation of all four involved functions. Beyond the training mode ψ, the distance from the learned multiplier to the analytical one is smaller than the distance from the BTCS multiplier to it. See the superiority paper for more interesting discussions of what happens if we combinatorially change ansatz, training reference, baseline, and even the PDE. This analysis, while restricted to linear PDEs and linear ansätze, reveals an interesting mech-anism. The inductive bias of the ansatz (here, the FTCS functional form) acts as implicit regu-larization, steering the learned multiplier toward the analytical solution even when trained on low-fidelity data.

emulator was trained at mode ψ but tested on other modes, so the improvement comes from generalizing across the state space. Superiority is not automatic; it requires a mismatch between the emulator’s inductive biases and the solver’s error structure (when the emulator has the same functional form as the training solver, θ∗ = γ2 exactly and no superiority occurs), a mismatch between training and testing conditions (here through different spectral content), and a sufficiently inaccurate training solver (if the training data is already close to the analytical solution, there is little room for improvement). However, there is a second form that does not require a change in initial condition distribution. 

Autoregressive superiority (ξ[1] ≥ 1 but ξ[n] < 1 for n ≥ 2) occurs even when training and test distributions are identical. The emulator accumulates errors more favorably than the numerical solver over multiple rollout steps (Figure 4.3). In our experiments (see Chapter C), autoregressive superiority appeared across all tested architectures (ConvNets, ResNets, FNOs, UNets, Transformers). State-space superiority, on the other hand, was observed primarily for convolutional architectures with local receptive fields. Global-receptive-field architectures (FNOs, Transformers) did not achieve it under the same distribution shift in our setup. These findings extend to nonlinear PDEs; for the full empirical analysis, see the publication in Chapter C. Superiority is a consequence of the interplay between solver imperfections and emulator structure. Since virtually all numerical solvers are imperfect (often intentionally so, to remain computationally feasible), the phenomenon is more broadly relevant than it might first appear. Whether superiority manifests in a given scenario depends on the precise experimental config-uration, underscoring the importance of understanding solver roles and careful benchmarking practices (Section 4.4). 

# 4.3 Efficient Differentiable Physics Training via Progressive Re-finement 

The previous section examined how solver properties affect emulators through training data (role (1) in Figure 4.1) as well as through architectural analogy (role (4)). A third interface is the coarse solver PH as a differentiable component for a neural-hybrid model (role (3)), see Section 3.2. Depending on the configuration, gradients must flow through PH either when training with the one-step supervised objective of Equation (3.2) or under unrolled training. Oftentimes, PH contains an iterative solver, e.g., for implicit time stepping (Section 2.1.2) or 

29 4. The Solver-Emulator Relationship 

for constraint enforcement (like the incompressibility projection (Chorin, 1968)). This creates a natural bilevel optimization structure: the outer problem learns the network parameters θ, while the inner problem is the iterative solver reaching convergence. The key insight, developed as part of Bhatia (2024) and, subsequently, the PRDP paper (Chapter B), is that full solver convergence is unnecessary for full network accuracy . This enables progressive refinement strategies that begin with coarse physics and adaptively increase fidelity during training. Note that this is distinct from manipulating the fidelity of the data-generating solver (1), which we discuss as an open research direction in Section 6.3.2. 

4.3.1 The Bilevel Optimization Perspective 

The bilevel view connects differentiable physics training to a well-studied problem in optimization. Pedregosa (2016) showed that when the outer problem (here: learning θ) is far from convergence, the inner problem (here: the iterative solver) does not need to be solved to full accuracy. Differen-tiable optimization layers Amos and Kolter (2017) formalize this further: any rootfinding or linear solve within the forward pass can be viewed as an inner optimization problem whose convergence tolerance is a free parameter. In our setting, the inner problem is the iterative physics solver with 

K iterations, and the question is how K relates to the quality of the gradients ∇θL that the outer optimizer receives. 

4.3.2 Progressive Refinement and Incomplete Convergence 

The gradient of the outer loss with respect to the network parameters depends on the accuracy of the inner solver. A more converged forward solve yields a more accurate solution, and a more converged backward solve (whether via unrolled or implicit differentiation, see Section 2.2.3) yields a more accurate Jacobian. In principle, both must be fully converged for exact gradients. In practice, two observations about neural network training relax this requirement considerably. 

Early gradients can be coarse. At the beginning of training, the network parameters are far from any optimum and the loss landscape is navigated in broad strokes. Precise gradients are unnecessary when the optimizer is making large, exploratory steps. This mirrors the classical insight from bilevel optimization (Pedregosa, 2016): when the outer problem is far from convergence, the inner problem does not need to be solved to full accuracy. In the differentiable physics setting, this means that starting with few solver iterations K ≪ Kϵ

and progressively increasing K as training matures can achieve the same final accuracy at lower total cost. We call the resulting savings progressive refinement (PR) savings. 

Full convergence is never needed. Even late in training, neural networks do not require exact gradients. Several factors contribute: (i) stochastic minibatching introduces gradient noise that typically dominates solver-induced inaccuracy, (ii) momentum-based optimizers like Adam (Kingma and Ba, 2015) normalize gradient magnitudes, reducing the importance of exact gradient norms, and (iii) neural networks are inherently approximative models whose final accuracy (on the order of nRMSE ∼ 10 −2) is far coarser than the tolerance ϵ required for solver convergence. Empirically, there exists a threshold Kmax ≪ Kϵ beyond which further solver refinement yields no measurable improvement in network accuracy. Training at Kmax throughout, rather than Kϵ, constitutes incomplete convergence (IC) savings. Taken together, PR and IC savings compound: starting coarse saves iterations early, and stopping refinement at Kmax saves iterations for the entire remainder of training. We illustrate this in Figure 4.4. The challenge is that neither the optimal refinement schedule nor Kmax is 

> 30

4. The Solver-Emulator Relationship Training epochs Validation loss Solver iterations  K   

> Coarse Medium Fine
> Kϵ
> Kmax
